# table — b1/c17

### #0597 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 597, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:52:18Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17", "scene_title": "One-Twelve, Before It Was Empty"} -->

**Scene b1.c17.s01 — One-Twelve, Before It Was Empty**
Location: Colma Notary & Mail, Mission Road — Friday, 7:25 a.m.
Cast: arenya, kino, abernathy

### #0598 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 598, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:52:32Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

**Friday. 7:25 a.m.**

The fog over Mission Road has come apart into thin strips, and the low sun lights them from underneath, gold, like steam over a pot. Across the road the Esposo yard is still chained, its granite blanks grey with dew. Two doors down, Flores de la Paz has its buckets out already, chrysanthemums and marigolds, and a woman in a fleece is hosing the sidewalk.

Appa parks the Corolla nose-out, the way he always parks, and leaves the engine running. "I am here," he says to the windshield. "Seven-forty, I come in. Before, if you wave." His hands stay at ten and two.

The roll-down gate of Colma Notary & Mail is down. Behind it the shop lights are on. Through the gate's slats you can see the counter, the wall of brass post-office boxes, and Rodel Panganiban in a cardigan sitting very straight on his stool. A big green clothbound register lies open in front of him, and he isn't reading it. He's looking at the door. When he sees you he gets up and goes around to the side, where a steel door opens onto the alley between his shop and the florist. He holds it open with his foot.

"Come in, come in. Don't stand in the street." He glances up at the strip-mall sign, then at the sky, like a man checking for rain. "Fifteen minutes. At seven-forty I open, because I always open at seven-forty, and today I am doing nothing I don't always do."

The box wall is warmer than the room. You both feel it when you pass, like standing near a stove that's just been switched off. Box 12 is at shoulder height, closed and ordinary. Box 112 is lower down, by the floor. Its little brass door is clean. Every other door on the wall has a film of dust and fingerprints. That one has been wiped.

Rodel puts his palm flat on the open register and doesn't sit down.

"Ask me," he says. "I was waiting for her niece to ask me. Ask me right, and I'll tell you right."

*(Bond, for Arenya: Ix is a pressure low against your shin, keeping away from the wall. "Brass again, love. Not hot. Warm. Like somebody's just took their hand off it.")*

### #0600 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 600, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:52:39Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I flip the notebook open on his counter, pen already moving, and keep my other hand well away from the brass.

"Mr. Panganiban. I'm Susmithaa's niece, and I'm not here to scare you. I'm here because you said a sentence to my father, and I'd like it in order. Who is *he*? You said he asked you for one-twelve before it was empty. Who asked, when, and how? A phone call, a note, in person? Did he leave a number, a name, or a hand you recognized? Start there. And tell me whose number is on the 2207 line, because it's yours."

I glance at Kino, then back at Rodel, and soften my voice a notch.

"Take your time. Just not fifteen minutes' worth."

### #0601 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 601, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:52:46Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I stay by the side door, shoulder to the frame, where I can see the gate, the alley, and the wall of brass at once. My eyes go to Rodel's hands, not his face. Then to the wiped door of 112, and back.

"Mr. Panganiban," I say, low. "She sat nine nights with your mother. Whoever *he* is, he used that. Tell it how it happened. Don't tell it the way you'd want it to have happened."

I touch the salt twist in my pocket, and nothing else.

### #0602 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 602, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:53:42Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Rodel doesn't answer straight away. He looks at Kino's hand on the pocket, then at Arenya's chin, the way he did on Wednesday. Then he bends under the counter and comes up with a different book. It isn't the green notary journal. It's a fat blue post-office ledger, BOX RENTALS 2014– on a strip of masking tape, the spine mended twice with packing tape.

"The journal's the State's," he says. "This one's mine."

He opens it to a page near the back and turns it around so it faces you. Each box has a row. Row 112 reads, in his own slanted ballpoint: **S. SOWRI — opened 04/2015 — cash, yearly.** Eleven renewals are initialled down the column. The last entry says **CLOSED 03/06/26, key returned, box emptied by renter.**

Above that line, squeezed into the margin in pencil, is one more sentence: *Hold 112 for Mr. Dalal on vacancy.*

"That's my pencil," Rodel says. "That's my book. I wrote it while he stood where you're standing." He puts one fingertip beside the line without touching it. "Look at it, anak."

The line is perfectly straight. Every other word on the page leans right, tired, a man who writes standing up. These letters sit on an invisible rule, and the final *y* ends in a small hook like a bracket.

"February. End of February, a Friday, around this time of day. He came in person. Nice shirt, from the temple in Fremont, he said. He knew her star. He knew she'd had a box here eleven years." Rodel swallows. "I can't tell you his face. I've tried at night. I get the shirt and I get his hands. Cold, like Mr. Bell's. He didn't sign anything or pay anything. He asked me to write it for him, so I did, and I thought it was *my* idea to write it neat." He taps the counter twice, hard. "Then he asked if the family could use my number 'for the paperwork,' since she trusted me. I said yes. I said yes, Mrs. Sowri's niece. That's how my number got on your line."

He looks toward the wall of brass. "Box twelve wrote to box one-twelve every March for eleven years. Long envelopes, no stamp, just put in. She took them home and never opened them here. One week after that man came, she emptied her box and gave me back her key, and she was angry. Not at me. She said, *'Rodel, they're moving in next door to me.'*" His voice catches. "I didn't understand. Thirteen days later she was back with your father's friend, notarizing her no, and an hour after her the polite one walked in and rented one-twelve with new twenties. He asked for it by number."

The clock over the stamp machine reads **7:29**. Outside, a car door shuts somewhere on Mission Road, and it isn't Appa's.

Rodel slides the ledger an inch toward Arenya. "Her signature closing the box is on the card in the back. The last thing she signed in this shop that wasn't a no." He leaves his hand flat beside it. "What do you want with it?"

### #0604 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 604, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:53:51Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I write "Hold 112 for Mr. Dalal on vacancy" in my notebook, then stop and look at the final *y*. I don't touch the ledger. I take out my phone, slowly so he sees it, and hold it over the page.

"Mr. Panganiban, I want a photograph of this page and of her card. Not a copy, not the book, and I'm not taking anything. Just evidence that your pencil is yours and his hand isn't. Is that all right with you?"

I tilt the notebook so he can see the bracket-hooked *y* I've drawn from the back of check 2207.

"This is the same hand that endorsed her check as 'R. Sowri.' You wrote his words and thought they were yours. That isn't a crime. It's what he does, and you're the only person who has told me so."

I look at the clock, 7:29, and toward the door where the car door shut.

"Who just pulled up out there? And before seven-forty: did he give you any instructions for today, on what to say or do when someone asks for one-twelve?"

### #0605 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 605, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:54:02Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I slide along the wall to the gate's slats without turning my back on Rodel, and look through them at the street. I check the car that shut its door: the plate, the driver, the hands. My left hand stays loose by my side, and my right stays near the salt.

"Arenya, keep the phone low," I say quietly. "Photograph it and step back from the brass. If anyone comes through that door, you're nobody's niece and nobody's signer. You're a customer."

I don't look at the ledger. I watch the door and the wall that was warm.

### #0606 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 606, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:54:21Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Rodel looks at the hooked *y* in her notebook for a long second, then gives one short nod. "Take your picture. Pictures aren't signatures."

The phone clicks twice. On the screen, the ledger page looks as it does on the counter, with one difference. Under *Hold 112 for Mr. Dalal on vacancy* there's a faint grey rule in the photo that isn't on the paper. It runs the full width of the page and off the edge, like the line under a column of figures before the total gets written. On the card in the back (*S. Sowri, box surrendered, 03/06/26*) her signature is firm and slanted. In the photo only, it's followed by a small round sun with short rays, drawn the way a child draws one.

Through the slats, Kino sees the street. A dark grey Nissan is parked at the curb two doors down, by the florist where the Camry used to sit, nose pointed toward Colma. It isn't Bell's car. The driver's door is shut and the engine is off. There's nobody in the driver's seat, nobody on the sidewalk, and nobody walking away. The windows are clean and dry, and on a cold morning they aren't fogged at all. Above the right rear wheel, a thin line of dew on the paint is perfectly straight.

"Instructions." Rodel wipes his palm on his guayabera as if it's still cold from Wednesday. "The polite one, Mr. Hale, called last night at 9:12 on the shop line. He said, *'If Ms. Sowri comes before I do, please tell her the box is open to her until seven-forty, she needn't sign anything, only show identification.'*" He says *identification* exactly as Hale would, carefully, and seems to hear himself do it. "And he said I should offer her coffee." There's a fresh pot behind him. Rodel doesn't remember making it. "I don't drink coffee," he says quietly. "I have tea."

The clock says **7:31**. Along the brass wall, the little door of 112 is shut and wiped clean. A thin warm shimmer rises from its keyhole, like heat over asphalt.

### #0608 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 608, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:54:32Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I lower the phone and don't look at the coffee. "Don't pour that, Mr. Panganiban. Not for me, not for you. Pouring is how it gets accepted."

I turn the photo toward Kino, thumb over the grey rule and the child's sun. "That sun isn't on the paper. Aunty's signature, and then a sun. Either she left it for me, or something is signing after her. I don't know which. Hold that thought."

Back to Rodel, quieter. "Here's what I want. Not the box, and not Hale's identification. He says I needn't sign, only show ID, but showing ID is still entering. So I won't. Write one more line in your own book, in your own slant, standing up: *Ms. Sowri came 7:25 Friday. Declined. Nothing accepted.* Then at 7:40, open the shop like always. If Hale comes, tell him the box is empty of me. Can you do that?"

I step back from the brass, one full stride, and drop my voice. "And I'll need Hale's 9:12 call in your words, as exactly as you can, because I'm betting he said it flat."

### #0609 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 609, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:54:43Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I keep my eyes on the grey Nissan. I note the plate and the straight line of dew over the rear wheel. Then I step back from the slats, away from the glass, and speak low.

"Nobody's in that car, and it isn't fogged. Someone is parked there who doesn't breathe. Rodel, is that the florist's customer lot? Do you know the car?"

I move to Arenya's shoulder, between her and the gate, and angle myself so I can see both the alley door and the wall. Then I take a pinch of Nana's salt and lay a thin line along the sill of the alley door, on the inside. I don't make a show of it.

"Seven-thirty-one," I say. "We say our piece, write our line, and walk out the side. Appa's nose-out. We don't wave early."

### #0610 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 610, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:55:06Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Rodel takes the carafe off the warmer and pours the whole pot into the bucket under the counter without looking at it. He doesn't spill a drop, and it doesn't steam as it goes down. "Not the florist's," he says to Kino, wiping his hands. "It's been there since before six. I saw it when I unlocked. Nobody got out."

He picks up a cheap blue ballpoint and writes standing up, leaning right, under the ruled pencil line in row 112:

*Fri 10/30 7:25 — Ms. Sowri came. Declined. Nothing accepted. — R.P.*

The ink goes on wet and ordinary. As he lifts the pen, the warm shimmer over the 112 keyhole thins and then stops, like a stove someone has turned off. Behind the brass door, something small and stiff slides down, maybe an envelope tipping over.

"The call." Rodel closes his eyes so he can get it right. "Nine-twelve. He didn't say hello. He said, *'Mr. Panganiban, this is Daniel Hale of Meridian Mutual. If Ms.'* and then he said your name, anak. Your whole name, every part, the way it's written on things. Then, *'comes before I do, please tell her the box is open to her until seven-forty. She needn't sign anything. Only identification. Offer her coffee. Thank you, that will be all.'*" He opens his eyes. "Flat. It wasn't polite like on Wednesday. It was polite like a recording of polite. And when he hung up, my phone said the call lasted forty-one seconds."

He taps the fresh ink twice, then stops. "There's one thing, though. Thirty-one years, and every line in this book has two hands on it. Mine and the renter's, or mine and a witness's. This one has only mine." He doesn't push the pen toward anyone, but he leaves it lying across the page. "I can let it stand alone. I don't know what that's worth to whatever reads here."

At the alley door, the salt Kino laid along the inside of the sill sits white and dry.

Through the front glass, the grey Nissan hasn't moved. Its passenger window is now fogged on the inside, one slow breath's worth, from the bottom up. The clock says **7:34**.

### #0611 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 611, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:55:19Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I look at the pen lying across the page, and I don't pick it up. Neither does Kino, and I catch his eye so he knows not to.

"Let it stand alone," I say. "A *no* doesn't need a second hand. It needs to be true, and it needs to stay where the book can see it. Aunty's no went in with one signature and a notary, and it's cut into a stone now. Yours is the same kind of thing. Nobody here adds a name to it, not me and not Kino. A witness gets entered with her household, and we can't pay that."

I close the notebook, and the weight in it is still there. "Mr. Panganiban, leave the pen where it is. Don't cap it. At 7:40 you open the gate like always, you say the box is open to no one, and you don't pour anything for anyone. If Hale shows up with that recording voice, give him your tea."

I step back toward the alley door, to Kino's salt line, with the phone still low. "One last question, then we go out the side. When a man writes through your hand, is there anything you can do to know it's happening? A taste, a chill, anything? Because if there is, Wednesday's you might have wanted to know."

I glance once at the fogged window of the Nissan, and not a second time.

### #0612 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 612, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:55:25Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I watch the fogged window one breath longer than Arenya did. Then I turn my back on it and put myself at the alley door, boots on either side of the salt line.

"Rodel," I say, quiet and steady. "Leave the pen. If anything asks for a second hand on that line, you say, 'Not today.' Out loud, in your own voice, and then you go make tea."

I tip my head at the glass. "It's breathing now. That means it's listening. We go out the side. Arenya first, over the salt, not on it. I go last. Nobody looks back at the car, and nobody says a full name."

I open the steel door with my foot, the way he did, and wait for her to walk through.

### #0613 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 613, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:55:46Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Rodel leaves the pen lying across the page, uncapped. He thinks about Arenya's question honestly, the way he'd think about a fee.

"It feels like being helpful," he says at last. "That's how it gets in. On Wednesday I felt *useful.* I haven't felt useful since my mother." He looks down at the line in his own slant. "Next time I feel that useful, I'll make tea first." He says *Not today* aloud once, under his breath, practicing it, and goes to fill the kettle.

The alley is cold and smells of wet cardboard and the florist's bins. Arenya steps over the salt, not on it, and Kino comes last, letting the steel door fall shut on its closer. Appa's grey Corolla is where he promised, nose out, engine running, hazards off. He has a thermos of Amma's filter coffee on the passenger seat and doesn't offer it to anyone. He looks at Arenya's face, sees that her hands are empty, and lets out a breath he has probably been holding since 7:25.

From the back seat, through the gap between the florist's van and a light pole, Mission Road is visible. At **7:40** exactly, by the dashboard clock, the driver's door of the grey Nissan opens. Daniel Hale steps out in his navy overcoat as if he'd only been sitting there. Nobody had been sitting there. He buttons the coat and walks into Colma Notary & Mail. Ninety seconds later he comes out holding a cream envelope by one corner. He doesn't look at it.

On the sidewalk he stops, turns his pleasant face toward the gap between the van and the pole, toward the back seat of a car he shouldn't be able to see, and nods once. It's a courteous nod, the kind you give across a lobby. Then he gets into the Nissan and drives toward Colma. The fog on the passenger window doesn't clear.

Appa's phone buzzes in the cupholder. It's a text from the shop line, Rodel in all caps like a man who learned texting late: **HE ASKED FOR TEA. I SAID NOT TODAY. BOX 112 CLOSED. NEW LOCK ORDERED.** A second text follows: **PEN STILL THERE. NOBODY TOUCHED IT.**

The sun is up properly now, low over the cemeteries.

### #0615 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 615, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:55:55Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c17.s01", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

**End of scene b1.c17.s01 — One-Twelve, Before It Was Empty**

Friday 7:25 at Colma Notary & Mail, Rodel showed Arenya and Kino his private blue BOX RENTALS ledger. Box 112 had been Susmithaa's for eleven years, and Bell's Box 12 left long unstamped envelopes in it every March. In late February a faceless man, "Mr. Dalal from Fremont," came in person, knew her star, borrowed Rodel's phone number "for the paperwork," and had Rodel pencil "Hold 112 for Mr. Dalal on vacancy" in Rodel's own pencil. The hand was ruler-straight with the bracket-hook, written through him. Susmithaa emptied and closed 112 on March 6 ("they're moving in next door to me"). Arenya's phone camera showed an unwritten sum-rule under the pencil line and a hidden child-style sun after Susmithaa's box-closing signature. Hale's instructions came through a flat 0:41 call at 9:12 the night before. The caller said Arenya's full name and told Rodel to offer coffee and ask only for ID. Arenya refused the box, the ID and the coffee, and had Rodel write in his own slant "Declined. Nothing accepted. — R.P." with no second hand. The 112 keyhole stopped shimmering, and the pen was left uncapped across the page. Kino salted the alley sill, and they left by the side door to Appa's car. At exactly 7:40 Hale stepped out of the grey Nissan that had sat empty since before six. He collected the cream envelope, nodded toward their hidden car, and drove toward Colma. Rodel texted that he refused Hale tea ("Not today"), closed 112 and ordered a new lock.

### #0617 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 617, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:57:48Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17", "scene_title": "The Long Way on 280"} -->

**Scene b1.c17.s02 — The Long Way on 280**
Location: Appa's Camry, I-280 south from Colma, Friday morning
Cast: arenya, kino, abernathy

### #0618 · dm → arenya,kino · narration
<!-- {"id": 618, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:58:02Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "kino"], "awaits": ["arenya", "kino"], "spotlight": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Appa doesn't take the freeway. He takes 280 the slow way, where the fog lies in the cuts between the hills like something poured and left to set. The wipers go on intermittent, then off, then on again. He drives at exactly the limit with both hands on the wheel, ten and two, the way he taught Arenya and the way she never does it.

He hasn't asked what happened in there. The thermos rides between the seats, steel and dented, and he still hasn't offered it.

At the Westlake exit he signals and pulls into a Chevron. The pumps are lit yellow against the grey. "Petrol," he says, though the needle is above half. He gets out, then leans back down into the open door and looks at Kino in the back seat, then at his daughter, the way he used to look at a breaker panel before deciding which switch to throw.

"Five minutes," he says. "I am going inside for the — " He gestures vaguely at the store. "Whatever they have." The door shuts. Through the glass they watch him walk past the pump, not stopping at it, and into the shop, where he stands in front of the coffee machines and doesn't touch any of them.

It's warm in the car. The engine ticks. Arenya's left palm is still cool, but it's only cool now, not counting. On the dash, the clock reads 7:58.

It's the first time since Wednesday that the two of you have been alone with no one listening who isn't supposed to be. *(Abernathy, in the bond, is quiet. He could speak. He chooses not to, yet.)*

### #0619 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 619, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:58:10Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I watch Appa through the Chevron glass, standing in front of the coffee machines like a man at a grave he can't find the name on. I keep my voice low.

"He's not buying gas. He's giving us five minutes." I turn the dented thermos over in my hands, not opening it. "Kino. Hale nodded at us. Through a van, through a pole, through the fog. He knew which window."

I get my notebook out and write the time, 7:58, then stop with the pen above the page.

"Here's what's bugging me. Hale's call was a recording of polite. The visitor writes through people. So is Hale a man, or is Hale a *form*, something the visitor fills in when he needs a hand?" I look at him. "And I said no three times in there and it felt great, and I don't trust that. Nothing's been that easy since Wednesday. What did we pay for it? You saw more than I did. Tell me what you saw."

### #0620 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 620, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:58:20Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Kino watches Appa through the glass for a while. The man stands in front of the coffee machines like they're a panel he hasn't been cleared to touch. Kino doesn't lean forward between the seats. He stays where he is in the back, forearms on his knees, and turns his left wrist over so the birthmark faces up.

"Your dad's giving us five minutes. I'm going to use them, because I've been carrying two things since Livermore and I'm tired of carrying them."

He keeps his voice level, the way he'd read a trail closure aloud.

"One. Your notebook. It's not just writing things down. When you say something true out loud, it gets heavier. I watched it happen at the meter, and again this morning, when you told Rodel *nothing accepted*. It isn't answering to the Lady or to Nana. It's answering to *you*. I should have told you Wednesday. I didn't, because I wanted to be sure. Being sure isn't my job. Telling you is."

He breathes out through his nose. His thumb rests on the spots on his wrist.

"Two. This one's harder." He looks at the fog sitting in the cut past the pumps, not at her. "For weeks now, every time I dream on the mountain, the Lady's there, and she's got her back to me. Not angry. Just turned. Looking at something I can't see. I haven't told Nana. I haven't told anybody." A short dry sound that isn't quite a laugh. "Twenty years of fasting and night-walks and I can't get her to look over her shoulder."

He finally looks at Arenya.

"I'm not telling you so you'll fix it. I'm telling you because you went into that shop and said no to a man nobody else could see. You're owed somebody who isn't keeping things from you, so I'm done keeping them." A beat. "Your turn, if you want one. Or we can just listen to the engine tick."

### #0621 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 621, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:58:33Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "awaits": ["arenya"], "spotlight": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

The Camry's engine ticks as it cools. A truck goes by on Serramonte and the windows hum with it, then go quiet.

The notebook lies open on Arenya's knee with *7:58* written on the line and nothing after it. When Kino says *it's answering to you*, the page doesn't move. It doesn't need to. She can feel the weight of it settle against her thigh, the way a phone in your pocket gets heavier when you know it's about to ring.

Kino's wrist stays turned up between them. In the flat grey light the birthmark is just a birthmark: four small brown spots, the kind you'd never look at twice. He doesn't cover it.

Inside the Chevron, Appa has picked a cup. He hasn't filled it. He holds it under the spout and looks at the buttons. The cashier says something to him and he shakes his head politely and keeps standing there. He's buying them more time and doing it badly, which is the only way he knows.

The fog in the cut past the pumps hasn't moved. It's the ordinary kind, wet and grey and full of eucalyptus. Nobody is sitting in it.

### #0622 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 622, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:58:35Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Kino takes the question the way he'd take a trail report: slowly, all of it.

"Three things I saw. The first one's the one that matters." He holds up a finger. "The Nissan's window fogged from the inside only while we were in the shop. Nobody breathing in there. So something was sitting in that seat and *keeping it warm*. Then Hale gets out at 7:40 like he'd been poured into the coat. A man doesn't do that. A form does. I think you're right. I think Hale's what gets filled."

Second finger.

"The nod. He didn't look for us. He looked at the gap. He knew where the car was because we told him. Rodel wrote it down in a book that isn't his, and Hale read it." He glances at the notebook in her lap. "Everything we write is a place somebody can find us."

He lowers his hand.

"As for what it cost." He's quiet a moment. "I don't think the no was free. I think it was *entered*. You said it true, so it counted, and what counts gets kept. Somewhere there's a line that says *Arenya Sowri declined*, and a clerk who never errs has it. That's good. It's also a name on a page the other side can read." He turns his wrist, the birthmark catching the pump light. "My cold patch is still a four. Yours stopped counting. I'd trust that less than I'd trust it hurting."

He looks toward the shop, where Appa still hasn't touched a machine.

"So when Hale's a form, who's holding the pen? Dalal's hook on the *y* ties him to the check. I want to know if that hand's got a *hurry*. Bell kept to the calendar, every March. This one moved up. Somebody got impatient, Ari."

### #0623 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 623, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:58:55Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I look at his wrist and the four brown spots for a long moment. I don't say anything clever. For once nothing clever comes.

"Okay," I say. "Your Lady first, because you went first." I close my thumb over the pen. "I don't know anything about mountains. But I've written about a lot of dogs, and a dog that turns its back on you in a doorway isn't ignoring you. It's standing between you and whatever's coming up the walk." I shrug, embarrassed. "That's a listicle, not theology. But you said she isn't angry. Maybe she's just busy *facing* something. Maybe it's the same something we are."

Then I look down at the notebook, *7:58* and the empty line, and it's heavy against my leg like it's waiting for me to say something true.

"My turn. The Tartine footage, the stuff that started all of this? I didn't get it with a press pass. Saiesh and I cloned Teddy's keycard. I made up a company project called 'Urban Mortality Patterns' and requested the footage under his credentials. His name, my hand." I let out a breath that isn't a laugh. "Do you get it? That's what Dalal does. He writes 'R. Sowri' on the back of a check. He writes through Rodel's pencil. He borrows somebody's number 'for the paperwork.' I did the exact same thing in March with a badge reader and a Google Form, because I wanted to be the one who found it. Not *a* person. *Me*."

I press the pen cap down until it clicks.

"So if the clerk keeps true things, it has that too. Somewhere under Teddy's name there's a line I wrote. I'd rather you hear it from me in a Chevron parking lot than find out the way Appa found out about the check." I glance at the shop, where Appa is still holding an empty cup under the spout. "And you're right. Everything we write is a place they can find us. Aunty said writing is not keeping." I look at the page, and then, slowly, I write anyway, in my own hand:

*7:58. Told Kino the truth. Nothing signed for anyone else.*

"If they're going to find me," I say, "they can find me there."

### #0624 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 624, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:59:12Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "awaits": ["kino"], "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

The pen drags on the last word as if the paper has gone thicker under it.

The new line doesn't look like the *7:58* above it. The time is in her ordinary quick hand. *Nothing signed for anyone else* sits darker and slightly indented, the way writing looks when it's been pressed through from the other side of the page. She didn't press that hard.

Then her left palm, the spot that went quiet past Glen Park and has stayed numb all morning, turns warm. It isn't painful. It's like a coin that's been held in someone's fist. One slow pulse, then it stays. Whatever had stopped counting her has started again, and this time it's a number she put there herself.

Kino's wrist lies between them. Under the pump light, for half a second, the four brown spots look less like a receipt and more like a signature, four marks with no name beside them.

Inside the shop, Appa has finally filled the cup. He pays in coins, counting them out onto the counter one at a time, picks up a second empty cup, thinks about it, and puts it back. The door chime sounds. He's coming across the lot with his head down against the damp, walking slowly enough to give them a few more seconds, and no more.

### #0626 · kino → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 626, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:59:20Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Kino doesn't pull his wrist back. He looks at the four spots, then at the dark, pressed-through line in her notebook, and something in his shoulders lets go.

"Thanks for the dog," he says. "It's better than anything I've got. Facing the walk." He nods slowly. "I'll take that to the mountain."

Then, quieter: "The keycard. Okay. You told it plain, and I'm not going to tell you it was nothing, because it wasn't. But you wrote it down knowing they can read it, in your own hand, with your own name. Dalal can't do that. He only signs as somebody else." He glances at the page. "That line didn't come out looking like a confession. It came out looking like a witness."

Appa's shape crosses the lot through the fog, cup in hand, head down.

"When he opens the door, say nothing about Teddy. Let him have his coffee. Tonight, though, when you can, tell Teddy yourself. Before somebody holding a pen does it for you."

Kino sits back, wrist turned down at last, as the driver's door handle clicks.

### #0627 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 627, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:59:32Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "awaits": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Appa gets in with the damp on his shoulders and the smell of burnt Chevron coffee coming in with him. He puts the cup in the holder and doesn't drink it. He checks the mirrors in order, left, center, right, the way he has done every time he's started a car for forty years, and pulls out onto Serramonte without a word.

The on-ramp loops them up into the fog, and then they're on 280 heading south. The hills on either side are just shapes. The reflectors tick past.

After a mile he says, to the windshield, "The thermos is rasam. Your amma's. I warmed it at five." A pause while the lane markers go by. "I didn't give it to you before because you were going in there. You don't eat before an exam."

Another mile. The wipers make one pass, though it isn't raining.

"Did it go okay?" He asks it the way he'd ask about a transformer inspection, plainly, ready for a bad answer. His hands are at ten and two. He doesn't look over. "You don't have to tell me what it was. Just whether it went okay."

In the back, Kino looks out the window and stays quiet.

### #0628 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 628, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:59:39Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I reach between the seats and pick up the dented thermos. It's still warm. I unscrew the lid and the smell of tamarind and pepper fills the car, and for a second I can't talk.

"It went okay, Appa," I say. "I said no. Three times. Nobody signed anything, and nobody got added to anything." I pour a capful of rasam and hold it in both hands. "Rodel's fine. He's scared, but he's fine. He's getting a new lock."

I take a sip. It's exactly as peppery as it's supposed to be.

"You were right about the exam thing. I'd have thrown up." I watch his hands on the wheel, ten and two. "And Appa? The 'it's handled' thing. I'm still mad about that. But you waited till 7:40 and you didn't ask me to stop. That was the right answer." I hold the cap out toward him, across the gearshift, the way I used to when I was nine and he drove with one hand. "Drink some. You haven't had anything since five."

### #0629 · dm → all · narration
<!-- {"id": 629, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:59:54Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Appa looks at the cap for a long moment, long enough that the car drifts a few inches toward the lane line. Then he takes it with his right hand and drives with his left, the way he isn't supposed to and always did.

He drinks it in one go and hands the cap back.

"Too much pepper," he says. "She always made it too much pepper." His voice catches on *always*, very slightly, the way a needle skips. He clears his throat and puts both hands back at ten and two.

He doesn't say anything about *it's handled*. A mile later, at the Crystal Springs exit, he reaches over without looking and turns the heater up one notch on her side. That's as close as he'll come to an answer, and they both know it.

Past the reservoir the fog thins and then gives way, all at once, to sun on the dry gold hills. Light floods the windshield. Kino shuts his eyes against it.

Arenya has passed this stretch before and felt everything go quiet: the dot, the dime, the cold. This time the warm spot in her palm stays. It doesn't fade at the county line. It doesn't flicker. It rides south with her, steady as a pulse, the one entry in all of this that's written in her own hand.

### #0630 · system → all · scene_end
<!-- {"id": 630, "ts": "2026-10-29T02:59:59Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_end", "scene": "b1.c17.s02", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

**End of scene b1.c17.s02 — The Long Way on 280**

At the Serramonte Chevron, while Appa stalled at the coffee machines to give them time, Kino ended his silence. He told Arenya her notebook grows heavier when she says something true aloud, answering to her rather than to the Lady or Nana, and that for weeks the Lady has turned her back to him in his mountain dreams. Kino argued Hale is a "form" something fills, since the Nissan stayed warm with no one breathing, and that Hale read their position from Rodel's book. Everything written is a place they can be found, and someone moved up Bell's March calendar out of impatience. Arenya offered that a dog turning its back in a doorway is guarding, not ignoring. Then she confessed she and Saiesh cloned Teddy's keycard and requested the Tartine footage under his credentials, "his name, my hand," which mirrors Dalal's method. She wrote "7:58. Told Kino the truth. Nothing signed for anyone else." The line came out darker, as if pressed from the other side, and her numb left palm turned warm with one pulse: a count she placed herself. Kino urged her to tell Teddy tonight, before someone with a pen does. Appa returned, revealed the thermos held Amma's rasam warmed at five, asked only whether it went okay, drank a capful Arenya offered ("too much pepper," his voice catching), and turned up her heater instead of answering about "it's handled." Past Crystal Springs, where the palm marks had previously gone quiet, the warm spot stayed, traveling with her.

### #0631 · system → all · scene_start
<!-- {"id": 631, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:01:35Z", "from": "system", "channel": "table", "type": "scene_start", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17", "scene_title": "Before Someone With a Pen"} -->

**Scene b1.c17.s03 — Before Someone With a Pen**
Location: Fri Oct 30, late morning → evening: the Sowri house, then San Francisco
Cast: arenya, malachi, abernathy, kino, ava, caleb

### #0632 · dm → arenya,malachi,abernathy,kino,ava,caleb · narration
<!-- {"id": 632, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:01:44Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "malachi", "abernathy", "kino", "ava", "caleb"], "awaits": ["arenya", "malachi", "abernathy", "kino", "ava", "caleb"], "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

*Movement — Friday, the rest of the day.*

Appa takes the exit without asking anyone. At the house the porch light is still on from last night, and Amma's chappals are still lined up by the door. He empties the thermos into the sink, rinses it twice, and sets it upside down on the rack beside her steel tumbler. He says nothing about either one. By noon he's asleep in his chair with the TV muted on a cricket replay, his reading glasses pushed up on his forehead.

Kino takes the couch for an hour and doesn't sleep. Around two he says he should check on Nana, and at the door he stops long enough to look at Arenya's left hand, the warm one, before he goes.

At **4:12 p.m.** Arenya's work email gets a message she wasn't copied on. It reaches her anyway, because Teddy forwards it with no comment except his corgi-gif signature:

> **From:** Records Compliance, SF Dept. of Public Health
> **Re:** Access verification — "Urban Mortality Patterns – SF 2025"
> *Our annual audit flagged a footage request under this project (Oct, Valencia St.). Please confirm the requesting party's authorization and the project's IRB status by* ***Monday, Nov 2, 5:00 p.m.*** *Unconfirmed requests are referred.*

Two minutes after that, a text from Teddy: **renny do u know what this is?? im at the office til 8, deadline hell. not a big deal prob 👉👉**

The palm stays warm. It doesn't pulse. Nobody has written anything yet.

*(Quick beats, if you want them: Arenya, what you do with the afternoon and how you get to the city. Kino, Nana's kitchen at dusk. Then we land wherever Arenya decides to have this conversation.)*

### #0633 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 633, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:01:54Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I read Teddy's forward standing in the hallway, between Amma's chappals and the muted cricket. Palm warm. Nobody has written anything yet. *Yet* is doing a lot of work.

I leave Appa his glasses on his forehead and put a note on the thermos rack: *Going to the city to fix something. Home by tomorrow. Eat the rasam. — A.* Then I text Teddy back, thumbs steady: **Not a big deal, but I need to tell you in person. Don't answer the DPH email. Don't forward it. Be there by 6:30. Please stay.**

I take the 5:00 BART from Daly City with the notebook open on my knee. I write the sentence I owe him before I can talk myself out of it: *I cloned your keycard. I made up the project. Footage request is mine. His name, my hand.* The line comes out dark and pressed. The palm stays warm.

At Saiesh's stop I almost get off. I text him instead: **I'm telling Teddy tonight. Whatever he does to me, you were never on the paper. Talk after?** Then I ride to the office with Nana's salt in my pocket, practicing the first word, which is *Teddy*, and the second, which is *I*.

### #0634 · malachi → all · action
<!-- {"id": 634, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:01:58Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I buy a prepaid phone for cash at a pharmacy on Mission, the cheap kind, and I don't put anything of mine in it. Then I take BART south and walk into Serramonte Auto in a rumpled coat, as a man shopping for a used Camry. I don't show a badge. I ask the sales manager one dull question: who bought a beige '09 with their frame on it, plate beginning 7K, and would he like a finder's fee for a name he'd give a stranger anyway. Cash, no log, no screen.

I take nothing from his desk. I watch his hands while he lies, and I remember what they do.

By evening I'm back in the city, on the bench outside Tartine with a paper cup, facing the door. I'm not there to be found. If Arenya comes, I'll know her walk before I see her face. If Bell's chrysanthemum man comes, I'd like to see him first.

### #0636 · abernathy → all · note
<!-- {"id": 636, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:02:05Z", "from": "abernathy", "channel": "table", "type": "note", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Abernathy rides the 5:00 BART beside Arenya, unseen. He speaks only in the bond, so I'm passing at the table.

### #0637 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 637, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:02:08Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I leave Arenya's couch at two with my boots already laced. At the door I look at her left hand one more time, the warm one, and say only, "Tonight. Before someone with a pen." Then I take the 14 and the bus home to the Excelsior.

Nana's kitchen at dusk smells of copal and onions. I wash my hands at her sink before I touch anything, the way she taught me, and check the salt line under the door with two fingers. It's unbroken. Ix lies across the threshold with his eyes half open, watching me and not the street. I kiss Nana's forehead and sit across from her.

"Nana," I say, in Spanish, low. "Two things. Whose Spanish does Bell count in? And the Lady has had her back to me for weeks. I should have told you sooner." I keep my hands flat on the oilcloth and let her see them. "Don't tell me it's nothing. Tell me what you'd do."

### #0638 · ava → all · action
<!-- {"id": 638, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:02:15Z", "from": "ava", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Friday, 6:10 p.m. Rush is thinning. I wipe the corner table twice, even though nobody's sat there since Wednesday, and log it in my head: *palm cold spot, 2 cm, unchanged; sleep, 3 hrs; caffeine, too much.*

Through the front glass there's a man on the bench with a paper cup, facing the door, not on a phone. Same coat posture as the napkin guy. I count: he's been there forty minutes and the cup hasn't gone down.

I pull a black coffee, no lid, and carry it out on my break with the salt twist in my apron pocket. I set it on the bench's far end and don't sit.

"Refill. House policy for people who've been staring at my door since the dinner rush," I say. "You're the CHP-log guy. Malachi. If Arenya's coming, she's coming from BART at the other end of this street. If the cardigan is, you'd better tell me what his shoes look like, because I'd like to see him first too."

I keep my left hand flat against my thigh, cold spot and all.

### #0639 · caleb → all · action
<!-- {"id": 639, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:02:19Z", "from": "caleb", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Friday, the motel south of Market. I eat half a vending-machine sandwich because my hands are shaking too hard to ignore. Both Vesper packs stay on the nightstand, unopened. The canister is against my ribs, ticking, and the folded thread on my forearm pulls toward the north-west, steady as a compass needle.

I sit on the edge of the bed with my boots on and say it out loud, practicing: "My name is Caleb Strand. I was in the grey jacket." It comes out flat. I try again and it comes out worse, which means it's real.

I don't go to her yet. I take the canister out, hold it a minute against the light, put it back, and watch the door until the sun goes orange.

### #0640 · dm → arenya,malachi,abernathy,kino,ava,caleb · narration
<!-- {"id": 640, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:02:44Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "malachi", "abernathy", "kino", "ava", "caleb"], "awaits": ["arenya", "malachi", "kino", "caleb"], "spotlight": ["arenya"], "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

**Serramonte Auto, 3:40 p.m.** The sales manager is Benny himself, with a gold chain and a lanyard. He takes the twenty before you finish the question. "Beige '09? Cash buyer, a lady, Asian, I didn't get a name." His right hand goes flat on the desk phone while he says *lady* and stays there. He doesn't pick it up until you're back on the lot. Through the showroom glass, Malachi, you watch him dial without looking at the keypad, a number his thumb already knows. Whoever he calls answers before the second ring. Benny says three words and hangs up. He doesn't look out the window, and that tells you more than if he had.

**Excelsior, dusk.** Nana listens with her hands around a cup of canela and doesn't interrupt. When you finish she's quiet long enough for the pot to tick. "He counts in whatever language the paper is written in, mijo. Here the paper is English. Mine wasn't, so he had to come to my kitchen to read it." She reaches across and turns your flat hands over, palms up. "The Lady. When a woman turns her back on you in the night, you ask what she is facing. You never looked past her. Tonight you sleep here, and you look." Ix shifts on the threshold and keeps his eyes on you.

**SOMA, the motel, 6:28 p.m.** Caleb, the thread on your forearm stops pointing and *pulls* once, hard, like someone on the far end has taken up slack. North-west, toward Mission and 18th or near it. The canister ticks twice against your ribs out of rhythm.

**The office, 6:31 p.m.** The floor is mostly dark. Teddy is at his desk with his coat on, his corgi mug full of cold tea, and the DPH email open and unanswered on his screen. He stayed. He turns his chair when the elevator dings and gives you a crooked half-smile that doesn't quite get where he wants it. "You said 'not a big deal' and 'please stay' in the same text. That's the scariest text anyone's ever sent me." He pushes the second chair toward you with his foot. "Okay. I'm here."

Your palm is warm. It hasn't ticked.

### #0641 · malachi → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 641, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:02:57Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I take the coffee and hold it without drinking, and I don't look away from the street.

"Malachi is enough," I say. "The CHP log is a story for later, and I'd rather you didn't repeat it where the glass can hear." A beat. "Cardigan, grey. Brown lace-ups, resoled, soft heels. He walks like a man who's never been late. But he isn't the one I came to see tonight."

I tilt my head very slightly toward the bench's far end, where the paper cup sits. "This afternoon a car salesman called someone before the second ring, and he said three words. I don't know who he called, and I know the call was about a beige Camry and a lady nobody names. So someone is already counting this street. You should go in and stand where the staff can see you. If a man in a dark wool coat or a grey jacket takes this bench after I leave, you don't pour for him."

I finally look at her, once. "Arenya's on BART, headed for a man with a pen on his desk. I'm waiting for her to finish. Then I owe her something I should have said a week ago."

### #0642 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 642, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:03:00Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I sit down without taking my coat off either. The two of us look like people waiting for a bus that isn't coming. I put the notebook on his desk, open to the page, and set my left hand flat beside it, the warm one, where I can feel it.

"Teddy." First word. "I did it."

That's the second word, and then the rest comes before I can rephrase it into a listicle. "The DPH email is mine. There's no Urban Mortality Patterns project. I made it up. In September I cloned your keycard, got into the vendor portal as you, and requested the Tartine footage from October on Valencia under your credentials. Your name, my hand." I turn the notebook so he can read it. The line is still darker than the ink should be. "I wrote it down on the train so I couldn't make it sound better here. I was going to make it sound better. I'm really good at making things sound better. It's literally my job, which you know, because you pay me for it."

I make myself look at him rather than the corgi mug. "I did it because people are dying. Nine of them since August, the same way, and nobody was looking, and I wanted to be the one who looked. That's the reason, and it's still not an excuse. It was your name on it. If this gets referred, it lands on you first, and you didn't know. So it shouldn't land on you."

My voice goes clipped. I let it. "I'll write to DPH myself tonight, or Monday, whatever you and legal want. I'll tell them it was me, no authorization, no IRB, the project's fake, you had no knowledge. I'll sign that one. It's the only thing I'm signing." I stop, then add, because it's true: "Someone did this exact thing to me this month. They used my name and their hand. I found out what it feels like from the other side. I didn't want you finding out from an auditor."

I take my hands off the desk and put them in my lap. "Okay. That's all of it. Except that I'm sorry, which comes last because it's the cheapest part."

### #0643 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 643, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:03:07Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

"English," I say, and almost laugh. "So he had to come to your kitchen to read it." I turn my hands back over and let Nana hold them a second longer than she needs to. "Okay. I'll look past her."

I eat what she puts in front of me, rice and black beans, because refusing would be an insult. Then I take the blanket she hands me and lie down on the floor of the front room, boots off, head toward the salt line. Ix settles against my back, warm and heavy.

I don't try to sleep and I don't try to dream. I do what I've done on the mountain since I was twelve: slow the breath, let the ceiling go, and ask. Not *why did you leave me*. I ask *what are you looking at, Lady?* I keep my eyes open as long as I can, and when they close, I try to turn my head the way she would, toward the walk, and not toward myself.

Somewhere across the city Arenya is saying the hard thing to Teddy. I think of her left hand, warm on a desk, and I hold my place.

### #0644 · caleb → all · action
<!-- {"id": 644, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:03:12Z", "from": "caleb", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

The thread yanks once and I'm on my feet before I've decided anything. I stand there with my hand clamped over my forearm and say, to the empty room, "Not like this."

Then I go anyway. I leave the Vesper on the nightstand, the canister against my ribs, and the jacket zipped to the chin. I wear a ball cap from the lost-and-found box and no grey. I take Muni, not the car, and get off six blocks short. I walk the last of it the way I was trained to: parallel street, check the parked cars for fogged glass, check rooflines, never the same side twice.

I don't go up to anybody. I come in toward Mission and 18th from the north and stop in the doorway of a closed laundromat, where I can see the corner, the café front, and the bench. Two people are on that bench side. I put my eyes on their hands, as I was taught. I keep my own hands out of my pockets and let them shake where I can see them.

If the woman on the wall comes into view, I'll know her, and I'll stay exactly where I am until I decide what my mouth is going to say.

### #0645 · dm → arenya,caleb,malachi,ava · narration
<!-- {"id": 645, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:03:37Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "awaits": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

Teddy doesn't say anything for a while. He reads the line in the notebook twice, his lips moving slightly on *his name, my hand*, then sets the notebook down square with the desk edge the way he does with contracts. When he looks up he isn't crooked-smiling anymore.

"You used my *badge*." It comes out quiet, which is worse than loud. "I defended that DPH email. I told legal it had to be a clerical error, because Teddy doesn't do that, because I'm the guy who reads the terms of service." He breathes through his nose. "Nine people?"

He doesn't wait for her to answer. He holds out his hand, palm up. "Your keycard. Not forever. Until Monday. You don't come into this building on anything with a magnetic stripe until legal and I are sitting next to you. And you don't write that letter tonight, alone, at midnight. That's how you got here." His jaw works. "We write it together. My name's on it too, as the guy who didn't know. That's the truth, so it goes in."

Then he turns his monitor toward her. Under the DPH email there's a pink while-you-were-out slip, photographed and forwarded from reception at 4:12 p.m. *Caller for Theodore re: footage request. Asked if he had "signed for it yet." Said he'd "wait for the signature." No name.* The callback number is a 650 number. Arenya, you know it. It's Rodel's shop line, borrowed again.

"I didn't call it back," Teddy says. "It felt like the kind of number you don't call back." He looks at her hand on her lap. "So. Who's waiting for my signature, Arenya?"

Your palm stays warm. It doesn't tick.

---

**Mission & 18th, 6:52 p.m.** Caleb, from the laundromat doorway you get both pairs of hands. The man's hands hold a full cup still, steady as a level, a professional's. The barista keeps her left hand pressed flat against her thigh like she's hiding a burn. Then the thread on your forearm goes **slack**. It doesn't fade; it's slack, the way a line goes when the person pulling it has arrived. Across the street the bench is still occupied by the same two people, and nobody else is there.

### #0648 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 648, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:03:48Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I slide the titanium-less, magnetic-stripe-less keycard out of my wallet, the real one, the one HR gave me, and lay it in his palm. "Monday. Fair." My voice does the clipped thing. "And together. Yes. Thank you. I don't deserve the 'together,' but I'll take it."

Then I look at the pink slip, and the 650 number, and my left palm stays warm and doesn't tick, which is somehow worse.

"That's Rodel's line. A notary's shop in Colma. A man borrowed it to put on a form with my name on it, and he's been borrowing it since." I tap the screen, not the number, the words. "'Signed for it yet.' Teddy, he's not an auditor. He wants you to sign something saying the request was authorized. Your name, his hand. Same trick, only now he's after yours." I breathe. "Don't sign anything. Not the DPH confirmation, not a 'verification' form, not a receipt, not a courtesy acknowledgement. Not for me, not for him, not for legal until legal's read it twice. If someone hands you a pen, you say you'll get back to them."

"Who's waiting? I don't have a face. I have a name that isn't his, a shirt from a temple in Fremont, and cold hands. I think he's been in my family's paperwork since March. I can't explain the rest in a way that won't sound insane, and I'd rather not lose you tonight for sounding insane." I try the half-smile and it lands about as well as his did. "Can I tell you the true parts and let you decide how much to believe? Starting with: it's not a prank, and I'm not in danger from you."

### #0649 · caleb → all · action
<!-- {"id": 649, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:03:58Z", "from": "caleb", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

The line on my forearm goes slack and I stop breathing for a second. Slack means the other end isn't pulling because it's *here*, or because something's cut it. I can't tell which, and I hate that I can't.

I don't move out of the doorway. I put my back to the laundromat glass, drop my chin, and run it again: the man on the bench with the full cup who hasn't drunk in an hour, the woman at the café door with her left hand pinned to her thigh like she's guarding it. Neither is the face off the wall. Neither is Shepherd's. I look for what's wrong with the picture. Nobody on the street is looking at them, which is wrong too.

I take the canister out of my jacket just far enough to read it by streetlight. Still ticking, still green. I tuck it away.

Then I do the thing I told the motel room I wouldn't. I step to the edge of the doorway, where the light just touches my boots, and stay there with both hands open at my sides. If the man on the bench is who I think he is, a man who watches doors for a living, he'll feel me looking. I wait for him to turn his head, and I don't speak first.

### #0650 · malachi → all · action
<!-- {"id": 650, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:03:58Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I don't turn my head. I use the Tartine window: a lost-and-found ball cap in the laundromat doorway across the street, a man who stands the way I was taught to stand, with his hands out of his pockets and shaking where I can see them. Whoever he is, he's showing me that he's unarmed.

"Ava," I say, low, eyes on the street. "Go in now, and don't look behind you. There's a man in the laundromat doorway. If he crosses to this side, you don't serve him and you don't speak first." I set the coffee down untouched.

I stand slowly, hands visible, and I cross to the middle of the sidewalk, no farther. I raise my chin once toward the doorway: *come out, or I come to you.* I keep my voice at the pitch I'd use across a dinner table.

"Whoever you are," I say to the dark, "you've been standing there longer than I've been sitting here. I'm not here to hurt anyone. I'd like to see your face before the next person does."

### #0651 · ava → all · action
<!-- {"id": 651, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:04:07Z", "from": "ava", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I don't go in. I take one step back, to the café's door, so the glass and the oven light and my coworkers are behind me, and I stay where I can see both of them. My hand stays pinned to my thigh. The cold spot is still two centimetres.

"Counter-proposal," I say, not loud, to Malachi's back. "I stay here, in the doorway, on camera, with a phone in my hand and a manager forty feet away. You two do the face thing. If that man starts a sentence with my name, I'm going inside and calling the police. I know how that sounds from a woman who's watched you refuse to use a phone."

I fish Kino's salt out of my apron and lay a short crooked line across the threshold, under the door's lip. Then I stand behind it.

"Nobody's pouring anybody anything tonight. But he's shaking, Malachi. Bell doesn't shake. Write that down for whatever you write things down for."

### #0653 · dm → arenya,caleb,malachi,ava · narration
<!-- {"id": 653, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:04:27Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "awaits": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "spotlight": ["caleb"], "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

**The office.** Teddy closes his hand around the keycard and puts it in his shirt pocket, behind the pen he always keeps there. He sees her see it, takes the pen out too, and lays it on the far side of the desk. "Okay," he says. "True parts. I'll decide how much I believe. That's the deal you're offering, and it's a fair one." He leans back. "Start wherever it starts."

She gets as far as *My cousin Susmithaa died on Monday* when the desk phone lights.

It doesn't ring at first. The line-one LED goes amber, and the little screen fills in by itself: **650**, then Rodel's number, one digit at a time, like someone dialing slowly. Then it rings. It's the old office ring, two short. Teddy doesn't move. Arenya, the warm spot in your palm **pulses** once, a single beat, in time with the second ring.

---

**Mission & 18th.** The man in the doorway steps fully into the streetlight. He's young, with a lost-and-found cap and a jacket zipped to the chin. His face is one Malachi has never seen, though he carries himself the way Malachi was taught to. Caleb, as you clear the doorway the canister against your ribs **speeds up**, *tick-tick-tick*, and it isn't aimed at the man in the middle of the sidewalk. It's aimed past him, at the café door, at the barista behind her crooked line of salt, at the hand she's holding flat against her thigh.

Ava, the cold spot on your palm goes from cold to *aching*, as if something across the street just called it by name without saying a word.

On the whole block, nobody else looks up.

### #0655 · arenya → all · action
<!-- {"id": 655, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:04:38Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I stop mid-word. Abernathy told me to, so I do, and I say it out loud instead of swallowing it. "My hand just ticked. On the second ring. Teddy, that's the number I was telling you about, and it dialed itself."

I don't reach for the receiver. I put my left palm flat on the desk between us, warm and counting, and keep my right hand off anything that holds a pen. "Don't pick it up. Don't say your name into it. Don't say 'yes,' don't say 'speaking,' and if it goes to voicemail, don't play it back tonight." I pull the notebook toward me and write while it rings: *6:58. Rodel's number dialed itself on Teddy's line. Nobody here is signing.* The line comes out dark and pressed, pushed up from the other side of the page.

"Let it ring out," I say, quieter. "I'm right here, and you're not alone with it. If you want to hold something, hold the pen. Just don't give it to anyone."

### #0656 · malachi → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 656, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:04:47Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I see his eyes go past me, to the café door, and I move without hurrying. I take two steps sideways until I'm standing in the line between him and Ava, hands open at my sides, my coat hanging loose so he can see there's nothing under it.

"Stop there," I say, evenly. "Whatever's in your jacket is ticking at her, not at me. I don't know you, but I know how you stand, so I'll ask the questions a stranger shouldn't be able to answer. Who taught you to walk a parallel street? And who gave you the thing in your jacket?"

I don't look at Ava. "Ava, step back off the salt line behind the counter. If that ache gets worse, tell me out loud."

To him, quieter: "You're shaking, and you came anyway, so you're not here to hurt her. Take your hand off the zip, show me what you're carrying, and tell me your name. I'll give you mine second."

### #0657 · caleb → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 657, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:04:55Z", "from": "caleb", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

"Caleb."

It comes out too loud, like a word I haven't used in a while, and I wince at it. I bring it down. "That's my name. You wanted the face first, and you've got it. It isn't much of one."

I stop at the curb and don't step off it. My right hand goes flat against my jacket over the ribs, pressing down, the way you'd press on a wound. Under my palm the thing is going *tick-tick-tick*, fast and wrong, and it isn't pointed at him.

"I'm not here for you." I say it to the man in the middle of the sidewalk, but my eyes have already gone past him to the woman in the doorway, to her crooked salt and her left hand pinned to her leg. "Ma'am. Your left hand. It just went worse, didn't it? Something called it."

A pause, too long. Fourteen months of nobody needing me to finish sentences.

"I'm carrying something that belongs to a dead woman. It started counting the second I came into the light, and it's counting at *you*. Not at him. At you." I swallow. "So don't come any closer to it. And don't let anybody touch that hand tonight who asks nice."

My eyes slide once, without meaning to, to the corner table inside the glass, the one by the window. I drag them off it.

"I'll stay on this side of the street," I say to the man, flat again. "You want to ask me things, ask. I'll tell you what I can. Some of it I don't remember. That part's not a lie. It's the worst part."

### #0658 · ava → all · action
<!-- {"id": 658, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:04:56Z", "from": "ava", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

"It's not ticking at you, it's ticking at me," I say, flat, case-report voice. "Palm: cold to aching in about four seconds, no external stimulus except him. So whatever he's carrying, it's reading my hand like a name tag."

I step back off the salt, one pace, behind the counter side of the door, and I don't let my eyes leave the cap. I hold up my left palm, spread, for anyone who wants to look.

"For the record, I'm not afraid of you. I'm afraid of the thing in your jacket. You can answer Malachi, or you can take it out slowly and set it on the pavement and kick it toward the curb. I'll write down either one."

I press my right thumb into the ache, because measuring is the only thing that works. "Name first, then the canister. Please. I've had a very long week."

### #0659 · kino → all · action
<!-- {"id": 659, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:05:04Z", "from": "kino", "channel": "table", "type": "action", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I sit up on Nana's floor with my palm still warm and Ix's teeth bared at the door. I say, "She's facing the walk, Nana. She's standing on it," and Nana nods once and starts pouring salt along the sill a second time.

I have my phone out before I'm on my feet. I call Arenya and let it ring through, and I leave a message, short, no names written anywhere: "It's Kino. The Lady never turned from me. She's standing between me and a line that counts my steps. Something at the far end felt me look. Don't sign anything tonight. I'm coming to the city."

Then I put on my boots and my black hoodie, take Nana's salt jar and a pinch of copal in a twist of paper, and tell Ix to stay with her. He doesn't like it. He stays.

### #0661 · dm → arenya,caleb,malachi,ava · narration
<!-- {"id": 661, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:05:14Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "awaits": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

**The office.** The phone rings eleven times. Teddy holds his pen in his fist like a kid holding a crayon and doesn't blink. On the twelfth ring it stops, and the amber light goes red: *1 new message*. The duration fills in on the little screen: **0:41**.

Arenya, you know that number. It's the same length as the call timer on the flip phone in the temple hall.

Teddy looks at the red light for a long moment, then pulls the phone's cord out of the wall jack, neatly, the way he'd unplug a kettle. "I'm not playing that," he says. "Not tonight, and not alone." His voice wobbles once and steadies. "Phones don't dial themselves, Arenya. I'm an analyst. I know that." He sets the pen down in front of *her*, not himself. "So keep talking. I'm deciding."

Your palm is warm again and quiet. On the next desk over, your cell lights up in your bag: **Kino**.

---

**Mission & 18th.** When Ava steps back behind the door, the canister doesn't slow. It *drags*. Caleb, it pulls against your ribs as if the ash inside leans toward the glass, and the ticking falls into rhythm with something. Ava, it's your ache, beat for beat. You're pressing your thumb into it, and the ticking across the street keeps the same time.

Ava and Malachi both see the canister's man's eyes go to the corner table by the window and get dragged off it. It's the table Ava has wiped twice a shift since Wednesday, the one nobody sits at. The camera above the register, the one whose October footage someone wanted badly enough to clone a keycard for, points straight at it.

### #0662 · arenya → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 662, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:05:23Z", "from": "arenya", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I look at the pen in front of me and don't touch it. I slide it back across the desk with one finger until it's square with the edge, his way. "Not mine to hold tonight. Thank you for the vote of confidence, though."

My cell lights up in my bag. I glance at *Kino* and let it ring out. "That's a friend. He's the only person who's told me the truth all week, and I'll call him back in ten minutes. He'd tell you to keep the phone unplugged."

Then I give him the true parts, in order, plain, like a timeline for an editor. "Monday my cousin Susmithaa died, in a car, in a lot, no cause but 'aneurysm.' Nine people since August went the same way: they freeze for under a minute, staring at someone, then they fall. In March somebody used my name to buy a prayer at a temple in Livermore, a paid one, an archana, in my star and my family's line. That put me in a book, as the one who's *insured*. Susmithaa was the one on the policy. I think the prayer is what made her go." My voice stays level, which is a miracle. "There's a man who writes in people's names. Ruler-straight handwriting. He calls and borrows numbers. He doesn't threaten. He asks you to *confirm*. Then you've said yes to something you didn't read."

I point at the dead phone with the cord hanging. "That was him asking you. And I think the footage I stole is the only reason he needs your name: because I put mine on a form he already wrote."

I keep my palm flat and warm on the desk. "I know how that sounds. So here's something that isn't a story: you pulled the cord before I told you to. You've got instincts that don't need me. Keep them." I take a breath. "Teddy, there's a voicemail on that line. Eventually someone should hear it, with two people in the room and nobody answering it. Not tonight. Can I stay while you decide?"

### #0663 · malachi → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 663, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:05:34Z", "from": "malachi", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

The name lands on me like a file I've been told is closed. I don't let it reach my face.

"Malachi," I say. "That's mine, as I promised. And I know yours, Caleb. I've been reading it on a motel register for two days." I keep my hands where they are. "Stay on your curb. You've done the hard part."

Then, to him alone, quieter: "I'm not going to ask you who sent you here tonight, and not with a civilian listening. Not yet. But that corner table, the one you looked at and dragged your eyes off: why? Did you sit there? Were you the grey jacket?"

I turn my head just far enough to speak to Ava without looking at her. "Ava, don't touch the table, and don't let anyone wipe it. Is the camera over the register recording?"

To Caleb again: "Put the canister down on the pavement, slowly, if it's pulling harder the nearer you get. Or walk it back across the street. Your choice, but it doesn't go into that café. I'll stand here till you decide."

### #0664 · caleb → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 664, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:05:34Z", "from": "caleb", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

"Army taught me the walk," I say. "Two tours, logistics. After that a contract outfit taught me everything else. I'm not proud of it."

I take my hand off the zip very slowly, the way you'd draw down on nobody, and open the jacket two inches so he can see there's no holster. Then I lift out the canister by its base with two fingers. It's a green steel thing the size of a thermos cap, ticking like a pulse that's lost its patience. I hold it low and away from the café.

"I'm not setting it on the pavement, and I'm not kicking it. Sorry, ma'am, I mean that. It's ash. It's a woman's ash, and she's been put on enough floors." My voice cracks on the last word, and I wait it out. "It's not a weapon. I think it's *her*. And it's reading your hand because the people who built it were reading yours. I don't know why yet."

I put it back against my ribs and keep my palm over it. It doesn't slow.

"Second name's the one you want, so here it is. I was on the team that did Tartine. I looked at that corner table through that glass in the light grey jacket I'm not wearing. And Monday I was on the team that took Susmithaa Sowri." I make myself finish. "I want to say that to her niece with my own mouth before anyone else says it for me. I'm not asking for mercy. I'm asking for ten minutes before you decide what to do with me."

Then, quieter, to Ava: "That table. Nobody sits there because it's wrong, isn't it? It's still counting."

### #0665 · ava → all · dialogue
<!-- {"id": 665, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:05:45Z", "from": "ava", "channel": "table", "type": "dialogue", "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

I hear "Tartine" and "Susmithaa Sowri" in the same sentence and my knees do something clinical. I put my right hand on the doorframe.

"Yes," I say. My voice is very even, which is how I know it isn't. "Nobody sits there. I've wiped it twice a shift and I couldn't tell you why. Observation, not theory: the oven light leans away from it."

"The camera over the register is recording. Motion-triggered, twelve-hour overwrite, the manager checks it Mondays. If anyone wants October off it, someone already asked. A man in a grey cardigan, in fact, though not out loud."

I look at Caleb. The shaking. The ash held low like a sleeping animal. I want to be angry, and what comes out is smaller.

"I watched a man die five feet from my steam wand because of that table. You were looking at it through this glass. I'm not going to tell you that's fine." I breathe. "Ten minutes. Not on this sidewalk, not near the window, and not in front of me alone. Malachi stays. Arenya's cousin was on that team's list, and her family is going to want your mouth, not mine. But I want to know what you saw before the paramedics came. Start with the four men's faces."

### #0666 · dm → arenya,caleb,malachi,ava · narration
<!-- {"id": 666, "ts": "2026-10-29T03:06:05Z", "from": "dm", "channel": "table", "type": "narration", "to": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "awaits": ["arenya", "caleb", "malachi", "ava"], "scene": "b1.c17.s03", "chapter": "b1.c17"} -->

**The office.** Teddy listens to the whole timeline without interrupting, the way he listens to a quarterly deck he suspects is cooked. When she's done, he squares the pen she slid back, puts it in his drawer, and locks the drawer.

"I believe you're scared. I believe that phone. I believe you'd have let me take the fall for a week if you'd thought it kept me out of this, and I'm angrier about that than about the badge." He rubs his eyes under his glasses. "Stay. We listen to the voicemail Monday, with legal, and with somebody who isn't either of us. Tonight I'm walking you to BART and you're calling your friend back." A pause. "And Arenya, I'm not signing anything. For anyone. I want that in your notebook too."

Your palm stays warm. Your phone shows a voicemail from Kino, 0:19, and a missed call.

---

**Mission & 18th.** Malachi walks them out of the window's eye: around the corner to the alley beside the bins, behind the café's back door, away from the register camera.
